Throughout we make the genericity assumption that
\kappa\notin\mathbb{Q}
.

Modules

Suppose that
\mathcal{W}
is a
representation of the Virasoro algebra
where
C
acts as
c\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}}
, and

L_{0}
acts diagonalizably with finite-dimensional eigenspaces
and eigenvalues with real part bounded from below.
Then
\mathcal{W}

has a unique structure of a module
for the universal Virasoro VOA
V_{c}
such that

\displaystyle Y_{\mathcal{W}}(\omega,\bm{\zeta})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\bm{\zeta}^{-n-2}L_{n},

i.e.,
\omega^{\mathcal{W}}_{(m)}=L_{m-1}\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{W})
,
see, e.g.,
[
LL04
, Theorem 6.1.7]
.

In particular any of the Verma
modules
M_{\lambda}=M(c,h(\lambda))
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
and their irreducible quotient representations

Q_{\lambda}=M_{\lambda}\,\big/\,M(c,\,h(\lambda)+1+\lambda),\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{N}

in Lemma
4.1
becomes a module for the
generic Virasoro VOA
V_{c}
.
We call these
Q_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}

first row modules
,
and we denote the module vertex operators in them simply by

\displaystyle Y_{\lambda}(\cdot,\bm{\zeta})\colon V_{c}\to\mathrm{End}(Q_{\lambda})[[\bm{\zeta}^{\pm 1}]].

Irreducible highest weight representations are
self-dual in the following sense.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be an irreducible highest weight representation
of Virasoro algebra, viewed as a module for the
universal Virasoro VOA
V_{c}
.
Then the contragradient module
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
is
isomorphic to
\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{w}\in\mathcal{W}_{(h)}
be a highest weight vector
in
\mathcal{W}
, and choose
\bar{w}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{(h)}^{*}

such that
\langle\bar{w}^{\prime},\bar{w}\rangle=1
.
From Lemma
4.3

we see that
\bar{w}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
is
a singular vector with highest weight
h
in
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
.
Then,
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
contains the subrepresentation
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{vir})\bar{w}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
,
which is a highest weight representation with the same highest weight
h
.
Since
\mathcal{W}
is irreducible, there is a surjective module homomorphism
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{vir})\bar{w}^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{W}
,
which in particular implies that
\mathrm{dim}((\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{vir})\bar{w}^{\prime})_{(\eta)})\geq\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W}_{(\eta)})
for all
\eta\in\mathbb{C}
.
On the other hand, by construction, we have
\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{(\eta)})=\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W}_{(\eta)})
,
\eta\in\mathbb{C}
.
Therefore we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{(\eta)})=\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W}_{(\eta)})\leq\mathrm{dim}((\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{vir})\bar{w}^{\prime})_{(\eta)})\leq\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{(\eta)}),\qquad\text{ for }\eta\in\mathbb{C},

and we can
conclude that
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{vir})\bar{w}^{\prime}=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\simeq\mathcal{W}
.
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

The first-row modules are self-dual,

Q_{\lambda}^{\prime}\cong Q_{\lambda}
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
.

Intertwining operators among highest weight modules

Let us now suppose that
W_{1}
,
W_{0}
,
W_{\infty}

are three modules for the universal Virasoro VOA
V_{c}
,
and that each of
W_{1}
,
W_{0}
, and
W_{\infty}^{\prime}

are highest weight modules
(note also that by Lemma
4.9
,
the contragredient
W_{\infty}^{\prime}
is a highest weight module for example if

W_{\infty}
itself is an irreducible highest weight module).
Let
\bar{w}_{1}
,
\bar{w}_{0}
,
\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}
be highest weight vectors in

W_{1}
,
W_{0}
,
W_{\infty}^{\prime}
, respectively,
and denote the corresponding highest weights
by
h_{1},h_{0},h_{\infty}
.

For an intertwining operator
operator
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{W_{1}\;W_{0}}
,
define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x}):=\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{Y}(\bar{w}_{1},\bm{x})\,\bar{w}_{0}}

and call this the

initial term
of the intertwining operator
\mathcal{Y}
.
The initial term defines a linear map

\mathrm{Init}\colon\;\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{W_{1}\;W_{0}}\to\,{\mathbb{C}}\{\bm{x}\}.

We make a few simple general observations in this setup.

Lemma 4.11
.

The initial term
of any
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{W_{1}\;W_{0}}

is of the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x})=A\,\bm{x}^{h_{\infty}-h_{1}-h_{0}},\qquad\text{ for some $A\in\mathbb{C}$.}

Proof.

Using Corollary
4.7
(2) for
n=0
,
Lemma
4.3
, and
the eigenvector equations

L_{0}\bar{w}_{1}=h_{1}\,\bar{w}_{1}
,

L_{0}\bar{w}_{0}=h_{0}\,\bar{w}_{0}
,

L_{0}\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}=h_{\infty}\,\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}
,
we calculate

\displaystyle h_{\infty}\;\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x})=\;

\displaystyle\braket{L_{0}\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{Y}(\bar{w}_{1},\bm{x})\,\bar{w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\;L_{0}\,\mathcal{Y}(\bar{w}_{1},\bm{x})\,\bar{w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\big(\bm{x}\,\mathcal{Y}(L_{-1}\bar{w}_{1},\bm{x})+\mathcal{Y}(L_{0}\bar{w}_{1},\bm{x})\big)\,\bar{w}_{0}}

\displaystyle+\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{Y}(\bar{w}_{1},\bm{x})\,L_{0}\bar{w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\Big(\bm{x}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}+h_{1}+h_{0}\Big)\;\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x}).

Thus the initial term satisfies

\bm{x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x})=(h_{\infty}-h_{1}-h_{0})\,\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x})
.
The solution space of this differential equation is one dimensional
and spanned by
\bm{x}^{h_{\infty}-h_{1}-h_{0}}
, so the assertion
follows.
∎

The following straightforward proposition contains a key idea,
that of an induction based on PBW-filtrations, so we do it in
detail here. In later proofs (including our main results),
we will then not always write out explicitly all of the cases,
as the ideas are similar.

Proposition 4.12
.

An intertwining operator
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{W_{1}\;W_{0}}

is uniquely determined by its initial term

\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x})
.
In particular,
by Lemma
4.11
we thus have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}\;\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{W_{1}\;W_{0}}\;\leq\;1,

i.e., if a non-zero intertwining operators exists, it is unique
up to a multiplicative constant.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x})=0
.
We will prove that then

\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})\,{w}_{0}}=0

for all
{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}\in W_{\infty}^{\prime}
,
{w}_{1}\in W_{1}
,

{w}_{0}\in W_{0}
. It will follow that
\mathcal{Y}=0
.
From this we can conclude that the initial term indeed
determines the interwining operator.

The proof of the above is done by induction with respect to
the total PBW word length
for the PBW filtrations of the
three highest weight representations

W_{1}
,
W_{0}
,
W_{\infty}^{\prime}
.
So assume that

\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})\,{w}_{0}}=0

whenever
{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{F}^{p_{1}}W_{\infty}^{\prime}
,

{w}_{1}\in\mathscr{F}^{p_{2}}W_{1}
,

{w}_{0}\in\mathscr{F}^{p_{3}}W_{0}
are such that
the total word lengths satisfy
p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}\leq p
.
Now let
n>0
, and consider any such
{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},{w}_{1},{w}_{0}
.
From Corollary
4.7
(2) we get that

\displaystyle\braket{L_{-n}{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}=\;

\displaystyle\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})L_{n}{w}_{0}}+\bm{x}^{n+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\binom{n+1}{k}\bm{x}^{n-k+1}\,\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}(L_{k-1}{w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle 0,

where each term of the second expression vanished by the
induction hypothesis
(note that also
L_{n}{w}_{0}\in\mathscr{F}^{p_{3}-1}W_{0}

and
L_{k-1}{w}_{1}\in\mathscr{F}^{p_{2}}W_{1}
above).
Similarly we get

\displaystyle\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})L_{-n}{w}_{0}}=\;

\displaystyle\braket{L_{n}{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}-\bm{x}^{-n+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}\;\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\binom{-n+1}{k}\bm{x}^{-n-k+1}\;\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}(L_{k-1}{w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle 0.

Finally, using Corollary
4.7
(1–3), we
similarly get

\displaystyle\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}(L_{-n}{w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{-n+1}{k}(-\bm{x})^{k}\;\braket{L_{n+k}{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\binom{-n+1}{k}(-\bm{x})^{-n-k+1}\;\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})L_{k-1}{w}_{0}}

\displaystyle-(-\bm{x})^{-n+1}\,\braket{L_{1}{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}

\displaystyle+(-\bm{x})^{-n+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}\,\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x}){w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle 0.

These three cases complete the induction step,
by establishing that

\braket{{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})\,{w}_{0}}

vanishes also whenever
{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{F}^{p_{1}}W_{\infty}^{\prime}
,

{w}_{1}\in\mathscr{F}^{p_{2}}W_{1}
,

{w}_{0}\in\mathscr{F}^{p_{3}}W_{0}
are such that
the total word lengths satisfy
p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}\leq p+1
.
∎

Intertwining operators among first row modules

To find all intertwining operators among the
first row modules
Q_{\lambda}
, in view of
Proposition
4.12

it suffices to determine when non-zero intertwining operators
can exist. We call the conditions for the existence

selection rules
.
2
2

2

These selection rules
turn out to take exactly the same form as the
selection rules which determine when an irreducible
representation of the quantum group
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
appears in
the tensor product of two others.

The singular vectors (
4.12
)
lead to necessary conditions.
Fix
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, and introduce the corresponding
polynomial of
h_{\infty},h_{0}

(4.20)

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(h_{0},h_{\infty})

\displaystyle:=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\geq 1\\
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{k}=\lambda+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-4/\kappa)^{\lambda+1-k}\;(\lambda!)^{2}}{\prod_{u=1}^{k-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{u}p_{i})(\sum_{i=u+1}^{k}p_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{p_{j}}\Big(\sum_{i>j}p_{i}+h(\lambda)-h_{\infty}+p_{j}h_{0}\Big).

Lemma 4.13
.

Suppose that
W_{0}
,
W_{\infty}

are modules for
V_{c}
such that

W_{0}
and
W_{\infty}^{\prime}

are highest weight modules
with highest weights
h_{0}
and
h_{\infty}
, respectively.
For each
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, the linear map

\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{Q_{\lambda}\;W_{0}}\to\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{M_{\lambda}\;W_{0}};\qquad\mathcal{Y}(\cdot,\bm{x})\mapsto\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{\lambda}(\cdot),\bm{x}),

where
\pi_{\lambda}:M_{\lambda}\to Q_{\lambda}
is the canonical projection, is an embedding of the space of intertwining operators.
Furthermore, this embedding is an isomorphism if and only if the highest weights satisfy the polynomial equation

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(h_{0},h_{\infty})=0.

Proof.

It is clear that for any

\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{Q_{\lambda}\;W_{0}}
,
the formula
\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{\lambda}(\cdot),\bm{x})
indeed
gives an intertwining operator of type

\binom{W_{\infty}}{M_{\lambda}\;W_{0}}
, so the
map is well-defined.

For its injectivity, observe the following.
The initial terms satisfy
\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}(\cdot,\bm{x})]=\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{\lambda}(\cdot),\bm{x})]
.
Now if
\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{\lambda}(\cdot),\bm{x})=0
, we have
\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}(\cdot,\bm{x})]=0
, which
by Proposition
4.12

implies that also
\mathcal{Y}(\cdot,\bm{x})=0
.
Injectivity follows.

The embedding is an isomorphism if and only if
all intertwining operators
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{I}\binom{W_{\infty}}{M_{\lambda}\;W_{0}}

factor through the irreducible quotient
in the sense that we have
\mathcal{Y}(w,\bm{x})=0

for all
w
in the maximal proper submodule of
M_{\lambda}
.
As in Proposition
4.12
,
we see that the factorization is equivalent to the
single condition
\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{Y}(S_{\lambda}\bar{w}_{c,h(\lambda)},\,\bm{x})\,\bar{w}_{0}}=0
,
where
S_{\lambda}\bar{w}_{c,h(\lambda)}
is the singular vector defined
in (
4.12
);
just note that the singular vector generates
the maximal proper submodule of
M_{\lambda}
, which
is isomorphic to
M(c,h(\lambda)+\lambda+1)
.

Let
\bar{w}_{0}
and
\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}
be highest weight vectors
in
W_{0}
and
W_{\infty}^{\prime}
, respectively, and
let
\bar{w}_{\lambda}
be a highest weight vector in
M_{\lambda}
.
Denote the initial term of
\mathcal{Y}
by

\displaystyle f(\bm{x})=\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x})=\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{Y}(\bar{w}_{\lambda},\bm{x})\,\bar{w}_{0}}.

By Lemma
4.11
, we must have

\displaystyle f(\bm{x})=A\,\bm{x}^{\Delta},\qquad\text{ where}\qquad\Delta=h_{\infty}-h_{0}-h(\lambda).

Again by a calculation using the formulas of
Corollary
4.7

(in fact the special case
{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}=\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime}
and
{w}_{0}=\bar{w}_{0}

of the last calculation in the proof of
Proposition
4.12
),
we get for any
n>0

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}(L_{-n}{w}_{1},\bm{x})\bar{w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle(-\bm{x})^{-n+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}\,\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})\bar{w}_{0}}-(1-n)h_{0}(-\bm{x})^{-n}\;\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})\bar{w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{-n}\,\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}({w}_{1},\bm{x})\bar{w}_{0}},

where we introduced the differential operator

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{-n}=\;

\displaystyle(-\bm{x})^{-n}\Big((n-1)h_{0}-\bm{x}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}\Big).

Recursively used, this allows to reduce the following expression to
a differential operator acting on the initial term

(4.21)

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}(L_{-n_{k}}\cdots L_{-n_{1}}\bar{w}_{\lambda},\bm{x})\bar{w}_{0}}=\;

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{-n_{k}}\cdots\mathscr{L}_{-n_{1}}\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}(\bar{w}_{0},\bm{x})L_{k-1}\bar{w}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{-n_{k}}\cdots\mathscr{L}_{-n_{1}}\;f(\bm{x}).

With induction on
k
,
starting from
f(\bm{x})=A\,\bm{x}^{\Delta}
and
using the explicit differential operators
\mathscr{L}_{-n}
, we find

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{-n_{k}}\cdots\mathscr{L}_{-n_{1}}\;f(\bm{x})=\;

\displaystyle A\,\bm{x}^{\Delta-\sum_{j}n_{j}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{p_{j}}\Big(\sum_{i<j}n_{i}-\Delta+(n_{j}-1)h_{0}\Big)

From the formula (
4.12
) for the
singular vector
S_{\lambda}\bar{w}_{c,h(\lambda)}
,
using (
4.21
), we get

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}(S_{\lambda}\bar{w}_{1},\bm{x})\bar{w}_{0}}=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\geq 1\\
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{k}=\lambda+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-4/\kappa)^{\lambda+1-k}\;(\lambda!)^{2}}{\prod_{u=1}^{k-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{u}p_{i})(\sum_{i=u+1}^{k}p_{i})}\mathscr{L}_{-p_{1}}\cdots\mathscr{L}_{-p_{k}}\;f(\bm{x}).

The vanishing

\braket{\bar{w}_{\infty}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}(S_{\lambda}\bar{w}_{1},\bm{x})\bar{w}_{0}}=0

therefore amounts to a differential equation for the
initial term
f(\bm{x})=\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}](\bm{x})
.
With the explicit formula
f(\bm{x})=A\,\bm{x}^{\Delta}
,
this differential equation simplifies to

\displaystyle 0=\;

\displaystyle A\,\bm{x}^{\Delta-1-\lambda}\;P_{\lambda}(h_{0},h_{\infty})

The intertwining operator
\mathcal{Y}
is non-zero only if
A\neq 0
,
and in this case the desired factorization is equivalent to

P_{\lambda}(h_{0},h_{\infty})=0
.
∎

For fixed
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
h_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
,
the equation
P_{\lambda}(h_{0},h_{\infty})
is a
degree
\lambda+1
polynomial equation for
h_{\infty}
.
It is possible to find the roots by a direct calculation
[
FZ12
]
.
With the quantum group method, the following easier argument
works as well.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Then we have the factorization

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}\big(h(\mu),h_{\infty}\big)=\prod_{\ell=0}^{\lambda}\big(h_{\infty}-h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)\big).

Proof.

Recall from (
4.10
)
that
h(\mu)
is a quadratic polynomial in
\mu
, specifically

h(\mu)=\frac{1}{\kappa}\mu^{2}+\big(\frac{2}{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}\big)\mu
.
Note also that

\displaystyle h(\rho+m)-h(\rho)=\;

\displaystyle\frac{m}{\kappa}\Big(m+2\rho+\big(2-\frac{\kappa}{2}\big)\Big).

Recalling that
\kappa\notin\mathbb{Q}
, we see in particular
that if
\rho\in\mathbb{Q}
, then
all
h(\rho+m)
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, are distinct.

First fix
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
\ell\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\lambda\right\}
.
Now if
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
\mu\geq\lambda
,
then we have

\sigma=\lambda+\mu-2\ell\in\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda,\mu)
,
so we may consider the non-zero vector

u={\iota}_{\;{\sigma}}^{{\lambda},{\mu}}\big(u_{0}^{(\sigma)}\big)\in\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\mu}
.
The associated function

\displaystyle F=\mathcal{F}[u]\colon\mathfrak{X}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}

satisfies the BSA PDEs
\mathscr{D}^{(j)}F(x_{0},x_{1})=0
, for
j=0,1
,
where
\mathscr{D}^{(j)}
is given by (
2.6
).
From the translation and scaling covariance and asymptotics given in
Theorem
2.2
it follows that
in this case the function must be simply

\displaystyle F(x_{0},x_{1})=B\,(x_{1}-x_{0})^{\Delta},

where
\Delta=h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)-h(\lambda)-h(\mu)

and
B=B_{{\lambda+\mu-2\ell},{\lambda}}^{\;{\;\;\mu}}\neq 0
.

But the BSA PDE for a function of the above form reads

\displaystyle 0=\;

\displaystyle B\,(x_{1}-x_{0})^{\Delta-\lambda-1}\;\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\geq 1\\
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{k}=\lambda+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-4/\kappa)^{1+\lambda-k}\,\lambda!^{2}}{\prod_{u=1}^{k-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{u}p_{i})(\sum_{i=u+1}^{k}p_{i})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\prod_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{p_{j}}\Big(-\Delta+\sum_{i>j}p_{i}+(p_{j}-1)h(\mu)\Big)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle B\,(x_{1}-x_{0})^{\Delta-\lambda-1}\,P_{\lambda}\big(h(\mu),h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)\big).

We conclude that
P_{\lambda}\big(h(\mu),h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)\big)=0
.
Now observe that
both
\mu\mapsto h(\mu)
and
\mu\mapsto h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)

are quadratic polynomials in
\mu
, so also

\mu\mapsto P_{\lambda}\big(h(\mu),h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)\big)

is a polynomial in
\mu
. We have just shown that this polynomial
vanishes for all integers
\mu\geq\lambda
, so it must be identically zero:

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}\big(h(\mu),h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)\big)=0\qquad\text{ for all
$\lambda\in\mathbb{N}$, $\ell\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\lambda\right\}$, $\mu\in\mathbb{C}$.}

Now let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{Q}
be fixed.
Consider the polynomial

h_{\infty}\mapsto P_{\lambda}\big(h(\mu),h_{\infty}\big)

of degree
\lambda+1
. From the above we see that

h_{\infty}=h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)
are roots of this polynomial,
when
\ell=0,1,\ldots,\lambda
. Since
\kappa\not\in\mathbb{Q}
,
these
\lambda+1
roots are distinct, and so we must have

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}\big(h(\mu),h_{\infty}\big)=\mathrm{const.}\times\prod_{\ell=0}^{\lambda}\big(h_{\infty}-h(\lambda+\mu-2\ell)\big).

From the defining formula (
4.20
)
one sees that the leading term of this polynomial of
h_{\infty}

is
(h_{\infty})^{1+\lambda}
, so the constant of proportionality above
is in fact
1
.
∎

The following theorem states that there is always a
nontrivial intertwining operator among an arbitrary
triple of Verma modules. The theorem can be seen as
a particular case of a more general one in

[
Li99
]
.
For self-containedness, we give the proof for the
case of our interest in
Appendix
B
.

Theorem 4.15
.

For
h_{1},h_{0},h_{\infty}\in\mathbb{C}
,

\dim\mathcal{I}{\binom{M(c,h_{\infty})^{\prime}}{M(c,h_{1})\;M(c,h_{0})}}=1.

The fusion rules among first row modules of the generic Virasoro VOA
now follow from Lemma
4.13
, Proposition
4.14
and Theorem
4.15
.

Corollary 4.16
.

Let
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}\mathcal{I}\binom{Q_{\nu}}{Q_{\lambda}\;Q_{\mu}}=\begin{cases}1&\nu\in\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda,\mu),\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall that the first row modules are self-dual (Corollary
4.10
).
We consider the following sequence of embeddings

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\binom{Q_{\nu}}{Q_{\lambda}\;Q_{\mu}}\to\mathcal{I}\binom{Q_{\nu}}{M_{\lambda}\;Q_{\mu}}\simeq\mathcal{I}\binom{Q_{\nu}}{Q_{\mu}\;M_{\lambda}}\to\mathcal{I}\binom{Q_{\nu}}{M_{\mu}\;M_{\lambda}}\simeq\mathcal{I}\binom{M_{\lambda}^{\prime}}{Q_{\nu}\;M_{\mu}}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{I}\binom{M_{\lambda}^{\prime}}{M_{\nu}\;M_{\mu}}\simeq\mathcal{I}\binom{M_{\nu}^{\prime}}{M_{\lambda}\;M_{\mu}},

where we used the symmetry in Proposition
4.5
. Then, due to Lemma
4.13
, the composed embedding is an isomorphism if and only if the highest weights satisfy the conditions

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(h(\mu),h(\nu))=P_{\nu}(h(\lambda),h(\mu))=P_{\mu}(h(\nu),h(\lambda))=0.

It is an easy manipulation of the factorization in Proposition
4.14
to see that these conditions
3
3

3

It can also be seen that two of these conditions imply the other one.

are equivalent to
\nu\in\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda,\mu)
.
∎

5.
Compositions of intertwining operators

Intertwining operators, defined in the previous section,
give
3
-point correlation functions of conformal field theories
(or alternatively, in a geometric interpretation, amplitudes in a
pair-of-pants Riemann surface with three parametrized boundary
components
[
Hua12
]
).
Importantly, they serve as the building blocks of more general
correlation functions. Namely, for
a multipoint correlation function (or an amplitude on
a Riemann surfaces with more than three parametrized boundary components),
one forms an appropriate composition of intertwining operators.

An intertwining operator is a formal series, with coefficients that
are linear operators between modules of the VOA.
Compositions of intertwining operators are then a priori formal series
in several formal variables, also with coefficients that
are (composed) linear operators between modules.
To get actual correlation functions, however, one considers
suitable matrix elements of the formal series of linear operators, and
crucially, one must then address the convergence of the corresponding
series.

In this section, we first study properties of the compositions of
the intertwining operators between modules of the first row
subcategory for the generic Virasoro VOA as formal power series.
Then we establish analoguous properties for the functions
obtained by the quantum group method —
specifically the ones corresponding to the conformal block
vectors of Section
3
.
The main result of this section
(Theorem
5.9
)
is that the formal series of the composition of intertwining
operators coincide with suitable power series expansions of the
actual conformal block functions, and that these series
are convergent in the appropriate domains.

More precisely, the section is organized as follows.
In Section
5.1

we fix our normalization of the intertwining operators,
and in Section
5.2

we introduce the specific spaces of formal series that
will be used.
Section
5.3

contains the proofs of two key properties;
that (the highest weight matrix elements of)
compositions of the intertwining operators
satisfy a system of partial differential equations,
and that the formal series solutions to this PDE system are unique
(up to scalar multiples).
In Section
5.4

we detail a recursive series expansion procedure for the
quantum group functions, and use this and the partial
differential equations to prove the main result
(Theorem
5.9
)
that the formal series of the composition of intertwining operators
converge to the functions corresponding to the conformal
block vectors.
In Section
5.5

we comment on some first applications of the
result.

5.1.
Fusion rules and normalized intertwining operators

A comparison of
Corollary
4.16

and Lemma
2.1

directly gives

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{I}\binom{Q_{\lambda_{\infty}}}{Q_{\lambda_{1}}\;Q_{\lambda_{0}}}=\;

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}\big(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{\infty}},\;\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{0}}\big)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }\lambda_{\infty}\in\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{0})\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Definition 5.1
.

When
\lambda_{\infty}\in\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{0})
so that nonzero
intertwining operators exist, we denote by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}\in\mathcal{I}\binom{Q_{\lambda_{\infty}}}{Q_{\lambda_{0}}\;Q_{\lambda_{1}}}

the unique intertwining operator normalized so that

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}](\bm{x})=\;B\,\bm{x}^{\Delta},

where

\displaystyle B=B_{{\lambda_{1}},{\lambda_{0}}}^{\;{\;\lambda_{\infty}}}\neq 0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Delta={\Delta}_{{\lambda_{1}},{\lambda_{0}}}^{\;{\;\lambda_{\infty}}}=h(\lambda_{\infty})-h(\lambda_{1})-h(\lambda_{0})

are as in
Theorem
2.2
.

The above normalization of the intertwining
operator
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}

is chosen so that that the “matrix element”

\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{\infty}},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x})\,{\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}}}=\mathrm{Init}[\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}](\bm{x})=B\,\bm{x}^{\Delta}

between highest weight vectors formally
coincides with the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{F}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](0,x)

constructed with the quantum group method from the
conformal block
vector
u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}\in\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{0}}

corresponding to
{\underline{{\varsigma}}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{\infty})}

as in Section
3
.

5.2.
Space of formal series

For a sequence

\underline{{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})\in\mathbb{N}^{N+2}
, and a
\underline{{\lambda}}
-admissible
sequence
\underline{{\varsigma}}=({\varsigma}_{0},{\varsigma}_{1},\ldots,{\varsigma}_{N-1},{\varsigma}_{N})
,
we consider the composition of intertwining operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}(w_{N},{\bm{x}_{N}})\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N-1}\,{\varsigma}_{N-2}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N-1}}(w_{N-1},{\bm{x}_{N-1}})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{2}\,{\varsigma}_{1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{2}}(w_{2},{\bm{x}_{2}})\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}(w_{1},{\bm{x}_{1}}),

which is a priori a formal series in
\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}

with coefficients that are linear
maps
{Q_{{\varsigma}_{0}}\to Q_{{\varsigma}_{N}}}
.
We consider in particular the “matrix element” between highest weight
states

(5.2)

\displaystyle C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N}):=\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1})\,{\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}}}.

As in the proof of
Proposition
4.12
,
the general matrix elements

\displaystyle\Braket{w_{\infty}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}(w_{N},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}(w_{1},\bm{x}_{1})\,w_{0}}

with
w_{i}\in Q_{\lambda_{i}}
,
i=0,1,\dots,N
,
and
w_{\infty}^{\prime}\in Q_{\lambda_{\infty}}^{\prime}
,
can be determined by a recursion
using the Jacobi identity (
4.18
) (in the
specific form of Corollary
4.7
)
from this matrix element (
5.2
);
this particular matrix element serves a role analogous
to the initial term of an intertwining operator. A priori,
(
5.2
) is
a formal series in
\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n}
with complex coefficients,
i.e., an element of
\mathbb{C}\{\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n}\}
.
But in fact the series
has a more particular structure:
it is essentially a formal
power series (with non-negative powers!) in the ratios of the variables.

Lemma 5.2
.

The series (
5.2
)
belongs to the space

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]][[\bm{x}_{N-2}/\bm{x}_{N-1}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}},

with
\Delta_{i}=h({\varsigma}_{i})-h(\lambda_{i})-h({\varsigma}_{i-1})
for
i=1,\dots,N
.

We make a preliminary to prove Lemma
5.2
.
For each
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
Q_{\lambda}(n):=(Q_{\lambda})_{(h(\lambda)+n)}
as the eigenspace of
L_{0}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
h(\lambda)+n
.
Suppose that a triple
(\lambda_{\infty},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{0})

satisfies the selection rule
\lambda_{\infty}\in\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{0})
, and take the intertwining operator

\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}

of type
\binom{Q_{\lambda_{\infty}}}{Q_{\lambda_{1}}\;Q_{\lambda_{0}}}
.
For
w\in Q_{\lambda_{1}}(k)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
using Corollary
4.7
(2)
for
L_{0}
like in
Lemma
4.2
,
we find that
\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}(w,\bm{x})

takes the form

\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}(w,\bm{x})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}w_{(m)}\bm{x}^{\Delta-m-1}\qquad\text{ with }\Delta=h(\lambda_{\infty})-h(\lambda_{1})-h(\lambda_{0}),

where
w_{(m)}Q_{\lambda_{0}}(n)\subset Q_{\lambda_{\infty}}(k-m+n-1)

for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We prove a slightly more general result than Lemma
5.2
:

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
w\in Q_{{\varsigma}_{0}}(n)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, the formal series defined by

\displaystyle C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N}):=\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1})w}

lies in the space

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]][[\bm{x}_{N-2}/\bm{x}_{N-1}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\,\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}-n}.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
N
.
When
N=1
, we have

\displaystyle\braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{1}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1})w}

\displaystyle\;=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{1}}^{\prime},({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(m)}w}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}-m-1}.

Here, since
({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(m)}w\subset Q_{{\varsigma}_{1}}(-m+n-1)
, the matrix element

\braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{1}}^{\prime},({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(m)}w}
vanishes unless
m=n-1
.
Therefore, we can see that

\displaystyle\braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{1}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1})w}

\displaystyle=\braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{1}}^{\prime},({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(n-1)}w}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}-n},

which is the
N=1
case.

Then assume that the assertion holds for
N\leq k-1
with some
k>1
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{k})

\displaystyle\;=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{k}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{k}\,{\varsigma}_{k-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{k}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{k}},\bm{x}_{k})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{2}\,{\varsigma}_{1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{2}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{2}},\bm{x}_{2})({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(m)}w}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}-m-1}.

Again, since
({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(m)}w\in Q_{{\varsigma}_{1}}(-m+n-1)
, the matrix element vanishes unless
m\leq n-1
.
In other words, we have the expansion

\displaystyle C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{k}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{k}\,{\varsigma}_{k-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{k}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{k}},\bm{x}_{k})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{2}\,{\varsigma}_{1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{2}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{2}},\bm{x}_{2})({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(n-\ell-1)}w}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}-n+\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}C^{\underline{{\lambda}}^{\prime}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}^{\prime}}\left(({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(n-\ell-1)}w;\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{k}\right)\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}-n+\ell},

where we set

\underline{{\lambda}}^{\prime}=({\varsigma}_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k},\lambda_{\infty})

and
\underline{{\varsigma}}^{\prime}=({\varsigma}_{1},\dots,{\varsigma}_{k})
.
By the induction hypothesis, each coefficient
C^{\underline{{\lambda}}^{\prime}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}^{\prime}}\left(({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}})_{(n-\ell-1)}w;\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{k}\right)
lies in

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[[\bm{x}_{k-1}/\bm{x}_{k}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{2}/\bm{x}_{3}]]\,\bm{x}_{2}^{\Delta_{2}-\ell}.

Consequently, we have the desired result at
N=k
.
∎

5.3.
System of differential equations

Due to the quotienting out of the
singular vector
in the first row
module
Q_{\lambda}=M_{\lambda}\,\big/\,\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{vir})S_{\lambda}\bar{w}_{c,h(\lambda)}
,
the matrix
element
C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})

satisfies a certain system of differential equations.
Given a sequence

\underline{{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})\in\mathbb{N}^{N+2}
,
we introduce the differential operators

(5.4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n}^{(j)}:=

\displaystyle(-x_{j})^{n}\left(-x_{j}\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x_{i}}-(1+n)h(\lambda_{0})\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n}\left((x_{i}-x_{j})\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x_{i}}+(1+n)h(\lambda_{i})\right)

for
j=1,\dots,N
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and

(5.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda_{j}+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\geq 1\\
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{k}=\lambda_{j}+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-4/\kappa)^{1+\lambda_{j}-k}\lambda_{j}!}{\prod_{u=1}^{k-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{u}p_{i})(\sum_{i=u+1}^{k}p_{i})}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p_{1}}^{(j)}\cdots\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p_{k}}^{(j)}

for
j=1,\dots,N
.
These differential operators
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n}^{(j)}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}

essentially correspond to how
the differential operators
\mathscr{L}^{(j)}_{n}
and
\mathscr{D}^{(j)}

of (
2.7
) and (
2.6
)
act on translation invariant functions; see
Lemma
5.7
for a
precise statement.
Notice that the actions of
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n}^{(j)}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}

on the space

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[\bm{x}_{N}]\,[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]][[\bm{x}_{N-2}/\bm{x}_{N-1}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\,[\bm{x}_{1}^{-1}]\;\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}

of formal series are canonically determined
in such a way that

\displaystyle x_{j}\mapsto\bm{x}_{j}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x_{i}}\mapsto\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\bm{x}_{i}}\quad\text{ for }i=1,\dots,N,

and the factors
(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n}

are expanded as the following formal power series

\displaystyle(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n}\;\mapsto\;\begin{cases}(-\bm{x}_{j})^{n}\,\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{n}{k}(-1)^{k}\,(\bm{x}_{i}/\bm{x}_{j})^{k}&\text{ for }i<j\\
\;\bm{x}_{i}^{n}\;\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{n}{k}(-1)^{k}\,(\bm{x}_{j}/\bm{x}_{i})^{k}&\text{ for }i>j,\end{cases}

i.e.,
(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n}\mapsto(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}_{j})^{n}
for
i>j
but

(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n}\mapsto(-\bm{x}_{j}+\bm{x}_{i})^{n}
for
i<j
.

The following standard lemma will be instrumental for us.
We include the details here to concretely demonstrate the
source of the specific power series expansions above.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\underline{{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})\in\mathbb{N}^{N+2}
be a sequence and
\underline{{\varsigma}}=({\varsigma}_{0},\dots,{\varsigma}_{N})
be
\underline{{\lambda}}
-admissible.
Then, the matrix element
C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})
solves the following system of differential equations

\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})=0\qquad\text{ for }j=1,\dots,N.

Proof.

Fix
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
.
For
w\in Q_{\lambda_{j}}
, consider

\displaystyle X(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N}):=\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{j}\,{\varsigma}_{j-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{j}}(w,\bm{x}_{j})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1}){\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}}}.

Then we can use Corollary
4.7
(1)
to calculate
X(L_{-p}w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})
, for
p>0
,

\displaystyle X(L_{-p}w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\phantom{+}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{1-p}{k}(-\bm{x}_{j})^{k}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots L_{-p-k}\,\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{j}\,{\varsigma}_{j-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{j}}(w,\bm{x}_{j})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1}){\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{1-p}{k}(-\bm{x}_{j})^{1-p-k}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{j}\,{\varsigma}_{j-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{j}}(w,\bm{x}_{j})\,L_{k-1}\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1}){\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}}}.

In the first term, we use

(5.6)

\displaystyle\big[L_{n},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{i}\,{\varsigma}_{i-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{i}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{i}},\bm{x}_{i})\big]\;=\;\Big(\bm{x}_{i}^{1+n}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}_{i}}}+\bm{x}_{i}^{n}\,(1+n)\,h(\lambda_{i})\Big)\;\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{i}\,{\varsigma}_{i-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{i}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{i}},\bm{x}_{i}).

from Corollary
4.7
(2), to commute
L_{-p-k}
to the left,
where it annihilates the highest weight
vector
{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime}\in Q_{\lambda_{N}}^{\prime}
.
The first term thus becomes

\displaystyle-\sum_{i>j}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{1-p}{k}(-\bm{x}_{j})^{k}\,\Big(\bm{x}_{i}^{1-p-k}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}_{i}}}+\bm{x}_{i}^{-p-k}\,(1-p-k)\,h(\lambda_{i})\Big)\;X(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle-\sum_{i>j}\Big((\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}_{j})^{1-p}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}_{i}}}+(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}_{j})^{-p}\,(1-p)\,h(\lambda_{i})\Big)\;X(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N}),

which is indeed expanded in non-negative
powers of
\bm{x}_{j}/\bm{x}_{i}
,
i>j
.
Similarly in the second term, by commuting
L_{k-1}
to the right and simplifying,
the commutator contributions become

\displaystyle-\sum_{i<j}\Big((-\bm{x}_{j}+\bm{x}_{i})^{1-p}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}_{i}}}+(-\bm{x}_{j}+\bm{x}_{i})^{-p}\,(1-p)\,h(\lambda_{i})\Big)\;X(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N}),

which is expanded in non-negative
powers of
\bm{x}_{i}/\bm{x}_{j}
,
i<j
. However, for
k=0
and
k=1
,
the highest weight vector
{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{0}}\in Q_{\lambda_{0}}

is not annihilated by
L_{k-1}
, so also the following two further terms
remain

\displaystyle-(-\bm{x}_{j})^{1-p}\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{j}\,{\varsigma}_{j-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{j}}(w,\bm{x}_{j})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1})L_{-1}{\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}}}

\displaystyle-(-\bm{x}_{j})^{-p}\,(1-p)\;\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{j}\,{\varsigma}_{j-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{j}}(w,\bm{x}_{j})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},\bm{x}_{1})L_{0}\,{\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}}}.

After simplifications (the first one still using
Corollary
4.7
), these two can be written as

\displaystyle(-\bm{x}_{j})^{1-p}\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}_{i}}}\;X(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})-(-\bm{x}_{j})^{-p}\,(1-p)\,h(\lambda_{0})\;X(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})

By combining everything, we get

\displaystyle X(L_{-p}w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p}^{(j)}\,X(w;\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N}).

Using this auxiliary observation, the assertion then follows easily
from the formula (
4.12
) for the singular
vector
S_{\lambda_{j}}\bar{w}_{c,h(\lambda_{j})}\in M_{\lambda_{j}}
,
and the fact that its canonical
projection in
Q_{\lambda_{j}}
vanishes,

S_{\lambda_{j}}{\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{j}}=0
.
∎

The coefficient of the monomial

\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}
in

\displaystyle C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\;\in\;\mathbb{C}[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\,\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}

is easy to trace in the calculations above. Ultimately
because of the chosen normalizations of the intertwining operators, this
coefficient is

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}B_{{\lambda_{j}},{{\varsigma}_{j-1}}}^{\;{\;{\varsigma}_{j}}}\neq 0.

In particular

C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})

is a non-zero solution to the system of differential equations above.

Theorem 5.5
.

In the space of formal series of the
form (
5.3
),
the solution space

\displaystyle\bigcap_{j=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ker}\,\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}\;\subset\;\mathbb{C}[\bm{x}_{N}]\,[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\,[\bm{x}_{1}^{-1}]\;\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}

of the above system of differential
equations is one-dimensional,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}\Big(\bigcap_{j=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ker}\,\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}\Big)\;=\;1.

Proof.

Since Lemma
5.4
ensures the
existence of a nonzero solution, it suffices to show
that solutions are unique up to a multiplicative constant.
We prove this by induction on
N
. When
N=1
,
observe that for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}\cdot\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}=P_{\lambda_{1}}\big(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+n\big)\,\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n-\lambda_{1}-1},

where

P_{\lambda_{1}}

is the polynomial (
4.20
).
Recall that by the genericity
\kappa\notin\mathbb{Q}
, we have

\displaystyle P_{\lambda_{1}}\big(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+n\big)\;=\;0

if and only if
n=0
. This shows that
\mathrm{Ker}\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}=\mathbb{C}\,\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}
,
and proves one-dimensionality.

For the induction step,
assume the uniqueness of solutions up to multiplicative
constants for
N-1
variables.
We regard

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[\bm{x}_{N}]\,[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\,[\bm{x}_{1}^{-1}]\;\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}

as a subspace of the space

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathbb{C}[\bm{x}_{N}]\,[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{2}/\bm{x}_{3}]]\,[\bm{x}_{2}^{-1}]\;\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}\bigg)[[\bm{x}_{1}]]\,[\bm{x}_{1}^{-1}]\,\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}

and on the latter we introduce a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading so that
the degree of a monomial is

\displaystyle\deg\big(\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}+n_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n_{1}}\big)\;=\;n_{1}\qquad\text{ for }n_{1},\ldots,n_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}.

The grading also gives rise to the corresponding notion of a degree of
an operator acting on the latter space.

Now let
C(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\in\bigcap_{j=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ker}\,\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}

be a non-zero solution. Expand it according to the grading above as

C(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})=\sum_{n=d}^{\infty}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}C_{n}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N}),

where
d\in\mathbb{Z}
is the lowest degree with a non-vanishing coefficient,

C_{d}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\neq 0
.
We express the action
of
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p}^{(1)}
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, explicitly as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p}^{(1)}=\;

\displaystyle(-1)^{p+1}\bm{x}_{1}^{-p}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\bm{x}_{1}}+(1-p)h(\lambda_{0})\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left((-1)^{p}\bm{x}_{1}^{-p+1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\bm{x}_{i}}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{-p+1}{k}\bm{x}_{i}^{-p-k}(-\bm{x}_{1})^{k}\left(\bm{x}_{i}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\bm{x}_{i}}+(-p-k+1)h(\lambda_{i})\right)\right)

in order to manifest the degree of each contribution.
Let us denote the first term in this expansion by

\displaystyle\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}}{\,}_{-p}^{(1)}:=(-1)^{p+1}\bm{x}_{1}^{-p}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\bm{x}_{1}}+(1-p)h(\lambda_{0})\right),

which is an operator of degree
-p
and
involves no other variables besides
\bm{x}_{1}
.
It is readily seen that, if
p\geq 0
, the
difference
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p}^{(1)}-\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}}{\,}_{-p}^{(1)}
is a
sum of operators of degrees strictly greater than
-p
.
Therefore, when we set

\displaystyle\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}{\,}^{(1)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda_{1}+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\geq 1\\
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{k}=\lambda_{1}+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-4/\kappa)^{1+\lambda_{1}-k}\lambda_{1}!}{\prod_{u=1}^{k-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{u}p_{i})(\sum_{i=u+1}^{k}p_{i})}\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}}{\,}_{-p_{1}}^{(1)}\cdots\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}}{\,}_{-p_{k}}^{(1)},

this is of degree
-\lambda_{1}-1
and the difference
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}-\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}{\,}^{(1)}
is a sum of operators of degree strictly greater than
-\lambda_{1}-1
.
Specifically, let us expand
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}=\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}{\,}^{(1)}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathscr{R}^{(1)}_{k},\quad\deg\mathscr{R}^{(1)}_{k}=-\lambda_{1}-1+k.

Recall the polynomial (
4.20
).
It is again straightforward that the action
of
\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}{\,}^{(1)}
on the monomial
\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, gives

\displaystyle\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}{\,}^{(1)}\cdot\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}=P_{\lambda_{1}}\big(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+n\big)\;\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n-\lambda_{1}-1}.

Having assumed the generic case
\kappa\notin\mathbb{Q}
,
from the factorization in Proposition
4.14
,
we see that
the factor

{P_{\lambda_{1}}\big(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+n\big)}

vanishes if and only if
n=0
.
This observation gives first of all an indicial equation:
the lowest degree term in
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}\,C=0
is

\displaystyle C_{d}\,\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}{\,}^{(1)}\cdot\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+d}\;=\;C_{d}\,P_{\lambda_{1}}\big(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+d\big)\;\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+d-\lambda_{1}-1},

and its vanishing is only possible if
d=0
.
Moreover, the same observation gives a recursion to determine the
coefficients of higher degree,
C_{n}
for
n>d=0
.
Indeed, plugging in the series expansions for both
C

and
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}
, the differential equation
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}C=0

for the solution
C
becomes

\displaystyle 0\;

\displaystyle=\left(\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}{\,}^{(1)}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathscr{R}^{(1)}_{k}\right)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}\,C_{n}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{\lambda_{1}}(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+n)\,\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n-\lambda_{1}-1}\,C_{n}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathscr{R}^{(1)}_{k}\left(\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}\,C_{n}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}P_{\lambda_{1}}(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+k)\,\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+k-\lambda_{1}-1}\,C_{k}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\mathscr{R}^{(1)}_{k-n}\left(\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}\,C_{n}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\right).

For each
k=1,2,\dots
,
the equality of the components of degree
-\lambda_{1}-1+k
gives

C_{k}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})=-\frac{\bm{x}_{1}^{-\Delta_{1}-k+\lambda_{1}+1}}{P_{\lambda_{1}}(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+k)}\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\mathscr{R}^{(1)}_{k-n}\left(\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}\,C_{n}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\right),

where we used the property that

P_{\lambda_{1}}(h(\lambda_{0}),h({\varsigma}_{1})+k)\neq 0

for
k=1,2,\dots
.
The apparent dependence of the right hand side on
\bm{x}_{1}
is checked to be cancelled by considering the degrees of the operators involved.
This formula implies that, for each
k=1,2\dots
,
the coefficient
C_{k}
is determined by the finitely
many previous coefficients
C_{0},C_{1},\dots,C_{k-1}
.
Recursively, we conclude that each
C_{k}
,
k=1,2,\dots,

is determined by the initial coefficient
C_{0}
.

Note that
C_{0}
belongs to the space

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[\bm{x}_{N}]\,[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{2}/\bm{x}_{3}]]\,[\bm{x}_{2}^{-1}]\;\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}

The next task is to show that the formal series
C_{0}
solves the
desired system differential equations. Those differential equations
involve the differential operators

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\prime}{}_{-p}^{(j)}:=\;

\displaystyle(-x_{j})^{-p}\left(-x_{j}\sum_{2\leq i\leq N}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x_{i}}-(1-p)h({\varsigma}_{1})\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq i\leq N\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{-p}\left((x_{i}-x_{j})\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x_{i}}+(1-p)h(\lambda_{i})\right)

for
2\leq j\leq N
. These differential operators

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\prime}{}_{-p}^{(j)}
do not involve the variable
\bm{x}_{1}
,
and they can be viewed either as operators on the space

\mathbb{C}[\bm{x}_{N}]\,[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{2}/\bm{x}_{3}]]\,[\bm{x}_{2}^{-1}]\;\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}

or as operators on the space

\big(\mathbb{C}[\bm{x}_{N}]\,[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{2}/\bm{x}_{3}]]\,[\bm{x}_{2}^{-1}]\,\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}\big)[[\bm{x}_{1}]]\,[\bm{x}_{1}^{-1}]\,\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}
,
in which case they obviously have degree
0
.
In the latter space, the difference
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p}^{(j)}-\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\prime}{}_{-p}^{(j)}

can be straightforwardly simplified to the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p}^{(j)}-\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\prime}{}_{-p}^{(j)}=\;

\displaystyle(1-p)(-\bm{x}_{j})^{-p}\Big(\Delta_{1}-\bm{x}_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}_{1}}}\Big)

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\binom{1-p}{k}\bm{x}_{1}^{k}(-\bm{x}_{j})^{1-p-k}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bm{x}_{1}}}-(1-p)h(\lambda_{1})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\binom{-p}{k}\bm{x}_{1}^{k}(-\bm{x}_{j})^{-p-k},

where the first line is a degree zero operator, and all the
terms on the second line have strictly positive degrees.
We thus see that

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{-p}^{(j)}\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}\,C_{n}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\bigg)=\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}\Big(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\prime}{}_{-p}^{(j)}\,C_{0}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\Big)+\mathrm{h.o.t.},

where
\mathrm{h.o.t.}
contains only terms of strictly positive degree.
Now the vanishing of the degree zero term in the
differential equation

\underline{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}{\,}^{(j)}\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}\,C_{n}(\bm{x}_{2},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\Big)=0

implies the differential equation

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}{}^{(j)}\,C_{0}(\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N})=0

for
C_{0}
, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}{}^{(j)}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda_{j}+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\geq 1\\
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{k}=\lambda_{j}+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-4/\kappa)^{1+\lambda_{j}-k}\lambda_{j}!}{\prod_{u=1}^{k-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{u}p_{i})(\sum_{i=u+1}^{k}p_{i})}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\prime}{}_{-p_{1}}^{(j)}\cdots\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{\prime}{}_{-p_{k}}^{(j)}.

The initial coefficient
C_{0}(\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N})
therefore solves the system of
differential equations
{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}{}^{(j)}\,C_{0}=0}
for
j=2,\dots,N
,
which is a system of the original form with one fewer variable.
By the induction hypothesis, such a
C_{0}
is unique up to multiplicative constants.
Since a solution
C
is determined by its initial coefficient
C_{0}
,
we conclude that the solution
C
is also unique one up to multiplicative constants.
∎

5.4.
Functions from the quantum group method

In this subsection we do the exactly analoguous
steps for the functions from the quantum group method as
were done for the highest weight matrix elements of
compositions of intertwining operators in the previous
subsection. The conclusions have an identical appearance, and
this is in fact what will allow us to show the equality of the two.
While Section
5.3

dealt with formal power series, we will now be working with functions.

We use the quantum group method of
Section
2
, with the difference
that we first use
N+1
variables, labeled
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
.
After some initial observations, we will in fact set
x_{0}=0
.

Translation invariance

We first note that the differential operators
\mathscr{L}^{(j)}_{n}

of (
2.7
)
preserve translation invariance.

Lemma 5.6
.

If
F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{X}_{N+1})

is translation invariant,

F(x_{0}+s,x_{1}+s,\dots,x_{N}+s)=F(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\quad\text{ for }(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N+1}\text{ and }s\in\mathbb{R},

then so is
\mathscr{L}^{(j)}_{n}F
,
for any
j\in\left\{0,1,\dots,N\right\}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let us set
\mathbb{L}_{-1}:=\sum_{i=0}^{N}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x_{i}}
. Then, a
smooth function
F
is translation invariant if and only if
\mathbb{L}_{-1}F=0
.
The desired result follows from the fact that each operator
\mathscr{L}^{(j)}_{n}
,
j=0,1,\dots,N
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
commutes with
\mathbb{L}_{-1}
.
∎

We introduce the
restricted chamber

\mathfrak{X}^{+}_{N}=\Set{(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N}\;\big.}{\;x_{1}>0}

of
N
variables.
To a given translation invariant function

F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{X}_{N+1})
,
we associate a corresponding function on
\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}
by

\widetilde{F}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}):=F(0,x_{1},\dots,x_{N}),\quad\text{ for }(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}.

For typographical reasons, when the function
F

is given by a lengthy expression, we
place the tilde symbol as a superscript,

\widetilde{F}=F^{\thicksim}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Fix
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
.
Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{X}_{N+1})
be a translation
invariant function. Then we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathscr{L}^{(j)}_{n_{1}}\cdots\mathscr{L}^{(j)}_{n_{k}}F\right)^{\thicksim}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n_{1}}^{(j)}\cdots\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n_{k}}^{(j)}\widetilde{F},

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

If
F
is translation invariant, then its
partial derivative w.r.t.
x_{0}
can be rewritten as

\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{0}}}F=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}F.

Consequently, we observe that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{(j)}_{n}F=\;

\displaystyle\phantom{-}(x_{0}-x_{j})^{n}\,\Big((x_{0}-x_{j})\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}-(1+n)\,h(\lambda_{0})\Big)F

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n}\,\Big((x_{i}-x_{j})\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}+(1+n)\,h(\lambda_{i})\Big)F,

which admits the specialization at
x_{0}=0
and gives

\big(\mathscr{L}^{(j)}_{n}F\big)^{\thicksim}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n}^{(j)}\widetilde{F}
.
The assertion is then obtained from
Lemma
5.6

by induction on
k
.
∎

The following simple corollary of this lemma
yields differential equations of the same form
as those in Section
5.3
.

Corollary 5.8
.

Fix a sequence

\underline{{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})\in\mathbb{N}^{N+2}

and a
\underline{{\lambda}}
-admissible sequence
\underline{{\varsigma}}=({\varsigma}_{0},\dots,{\varsigma}_{N})
.
Let
u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}\in\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}\big)\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{0}}

be the corresponding conformal block
vector (
3.5
),
and let

F=\mathcal{F}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}]\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{X}_{N+1})

denote the corresponding function obtained by the quantum group method
of Section
2.2
.
Then
F
is translation invariant, and
the associated function
\widetilde{F}
on
\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}

solves the system of differential equations

\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}\widetilde{F}=0
,
j=1,\dots,N
.

Series expansions of the functions

Fix again a sequence

\underline{{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})\in\mathbb{N}^{N+2}

and a
\underline{{\lambda}}
-admissible sequence
\underline{{\varsigma}}=({\varsigma}_{0},\dots,{\varsigma}_{N})
,
and let
\widetilde{F}\colon\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
be the function given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\;=\;\mathcal{F}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](0,x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\qquad\text{ for }(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}

where
u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}\in\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}\big)\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{0}}

is the corresponding conformal block vector
of (
3.5
).
We will expand this function as a series recursively, one variable at a time,
starting from
x_{1}
.
The series expansion in
x_{1}
is the Frobenius series at
0

given by Lemma
2.5
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\;=\;x_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}\,\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{N})\,x_{1}^{k},

and the other variables

(x_{2},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+}

are treated as parameters. When the other variables stay in the open subset

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+;R}:=\left\{(x_{2},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+}\;\Big|\;x_{2}>R\right\},

the Frobenius series in locally uniformly
R
-controlled.

The differential operators
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n}^{(j)}

and
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}

of (
5.4
)
and (
5.5
)
are composed of

•

differentiations
\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{1}}}
with respect to
the power series variable
x_{1}
;

•

differentiations
\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}

with respect to the parameters
x_{i}
, for
i=2,\ldots,N
;

•

multiplication operators by
(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n}

for
i\neq j
and by
x_{i}^{n}
.

If
i,j>1
, then the multiplication by
(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n}
acts on
the coefficients
c_{k}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+;R})

by a multiplication by a smooth (and in particular locally
bounded) function. By contrast
{(x_{i}-x_{1})^{n}}
for
i=2,\ldots,N

is expanded as a power series

(x_{i}-x_{1})^{n}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\,\binom{n}{j}\,x_{i}^{n-j}\,x_{1}^{j}
,
which is itself locally uniformly
R
-controlled power series in
x_{1}
on

\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+;R}
, and the multiplication operator
involves a convolution of the coefficients of the power series.

By Lemma
A.1
, each one
of the above constituent operators preserves the space of
locally uniformly
R
-controlled series and acts naturally
coefficientwise when
(x_{2},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+;R}
.
Therefore the constituent
operators can be composed, and the operators
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n}^{(j)}

and
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}
also act on the space of
locally uniformly
R
-controlled series, and they also act naturally
coefficientwise, when
(x_{2},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+;R}
.
For different values of
R>0
, the actions of
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n}^{(j)}

and
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(j)}
on
power series parametrized by
\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+;R}

are consistent (coefficient functions are obtained by restrictions
to the smaller subset), so they give rise to
a natural action on power series
parametrized simply by
\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+}=\bigcup_{R>0}\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+;R}
.

It will turn out in the analysis below that the coefficient
functions
c_{k}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+})

are themselves (essentially) given by functions from the quantum group
method, and they therefore admit Frobenius series expansions of their
own. Recursively in the number of variables, this allows us to
uniquely associate to
\widetilde{F}
a power series in
the space (
5.3
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[\bm{x}_{N}]\,[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\,[\bm{x}_{1}^{-1}]\;\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}.

The following is our main result about the equality of the
quantum group functions and (the highest weight matrix elements of) the
compositions of intertwining
operators of the first row subcategory of the generic Virasoro VOA.

Theorem 5.9
.

For any

\underline{{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})\in\mathbb{N}^{N+2}

and any
\underline{{\lambda}}
-admissible sequence
\underline{{\varsigma}}=({\varsigma}_{0},\dots,{\varsigma}_{N})
,
the function
\mathcal{F}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}]
admits a series expansion

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](0,x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}}\,x_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+k_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{\Delta_{N}+k_{N}},

which is convergent for any
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}
.
As a formal series, this coincides with
the matrix element (
5.2
)
of the composition of intertwining operators,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](0,\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N})=\;

\displaystyle C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{N})\;\in\;\mathbb{C}[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\,\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}.

Proof.

We employ an induction over the number of variables
N
.
The case of
N=1
is obvious; the function is

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](x)=B_{{\lambda_{1}},{\lambda_{0}}}^{\;{\lambda_{\infty}}}x^{\Delta_{1}},\quad x>0.

This function gives the same one term power series
in
\mathbb{C}\bm{x}^{\Delta_{1}}
as the matrix
element
C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}(\bm{x})
.

Assume that the assertion is true
when the number of variables is strictly less than
N
.
Then use Lemma
2.5

to obtain a Frobenius expansion
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}]
at
x_{1}=0
,

(5.8)

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{F}_{n}(x_{2},\dots,x_{N})x_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+n}

Note that by Remark
2.6
,
the initial coefficient
\widetilde{F}_{0}
coincides with

B_{{\lambda_{1}},{\lambda_{0}}}^{\;{{\varsigma}_{1}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}^{\prime}}]
,
where
{\underline{{\varsigma}}^{\prime}=({\varsigma}_{1},\dots,{\varsigma}_{N})}

is a
({\varsigma}_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})
-admissible
sequence.

We apply
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{(1)}
to both sides of the expansion (
5.8
).
Because the operators act coefficientwise, we may
employ similar arguments as in the proof of Theorem
5.5
.
We find that
\widetilde{F}_{n}
,
n\geq 1
,
are determined recursively as

\widetilde{F}_{n}(x_{2},\dots,x_{N})=-\frac{\bm{x}_{1}^{-\Delta_{1}-n+\lambda_{1}+1}}{P_{\lambda_{1}}(h(\lambda_{0}),h(\varsigma_{1})+n)}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\mathscr{R}^{(1)}_{n-m}\left(\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+m}\widetilde{F}_{m}(x_{2},\dots,x_{N})\right),

where
\mathscr{R}^{(1)}_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, are differential
operators given by exactly the same formulas as in the proof of
Theorem
5.5
,
but now acting on the
space
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+})[[\bm{x}_{1}^{\pm 1}]]\,\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}
.

In particular,
\widetilde{F}_{n}
,
n\geq 1
, are
determined by
\widetilde{F}_{0}
via the same relations as
C_{n}
,

n\geq 1
, are determined by
C_{0}
.
It also follows that all higher coefficients
F_{n}
,
n\geq 1
, are
analytic on
\mathfrak{X}_{N-1}^{+}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\widetilde{F}_{0}
is expanded in

\mathbb{C}[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{2}/\bm{x}_{3}]]\,\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}

and coincides with
C_{0}
.
Therefore, each
\widetilde{F}_{n}
,
n\geq 0
is expanded in

\mathbb{C}[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{2}/\bm{x}_{3}]]\,\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{2}^{\Delta_{2}-n}

and coincides with
C_{n}
.
Finally, from the expansion (
5.8
), we conclude that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}]
is expanded in

\mathbb{C}[[\bm{x}_{N-1}/\bm{x}_{N}]]\cdots[[\bm{x}_{1}/\bm{x}_{2}]]\,\bm{x}_{N}^{\Delta_{N}}\cdots\bm{x}_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}

and coincides with
C^{\underline{{\lambda}}}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}
.
The convergence of this series at any

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}

is clear by an inductive application of
Lemma
2.5
.
∎

5.5.
Some applications

Let us quickly comment on applications of the above result to
the analysis of the PDE system and to the quantum group method itself.

On the solution spaces to BPZ differential equations

By Theorem
5.9
,
for every
u\in\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}\big)\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{0}}

such that
Eu=0
we associate a formal series
representing the function
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}[u]\colon\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}[u]\;\in\;\mathbb{C}\{\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}.

By convergence of the series
(pointwise) in
\mathfrak{X}_{N}^{+}
, the series uniquely determines
the function
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}[u]
, and by translation invariance it therefore also
determines
\mathcal{F}[u]\colon\mathfrak{X}_{N+1}\to\mathbb{C}
.

For fixed

\underline{{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})\in\mathbb{N}^{N+2}
,
any
\underline{{\lambda}}
-admissible sequence
\underline{{\varsigma}}=({\varsigma}_{0},\dots,{\varsigma}_{N})

gives a solution to the (same) system of BPZ partial differential equations
of BSA form,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j)}F=0\qquad\text{ for }j=0,\ldots,N,

with homogeneity

(5.10)

\displaystyle F(sx_{0},sx_{1},\ldots,sx_{N})=s^{h(\lambda_{\infty})-\sum_{j=0}^{N}h(\lambda_{j})}\,F(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})

for any
s>0
and
(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{N+1}
.

For different
\underline{{\varsigma}}
,
the corresponding formal series in

\mathbb{C}\{\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}

are clearly linearly independent, since the sequences

(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{N})
of exponents

\Delta_{i}=h({\varsigma}_{i})-h(\lambda_{i})-h({\varsigma}_{i-1})
,

i=1,\ldots,N
, are distinct in the generic
case. In particular the conformal block type
solutions
\mathcal{F}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}]
, for
\underline{{\varsigma}}
admissible,
are linearly independent solutions to the system of differential
equations.

As an application, we therefore get that the dimension of the solution
space is at least the number of conformal blocks,
which is a combinatorial and well understood quantity.

Corollary 5.10
.

Let

\underline{{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N},\lambda_{\infty})\in\mathbb{N}^{N+2}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}\left\{F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{X}_{N+1})\;\bigg|\;F\text{ satisfies \eqref{eq: BPZ PDE system of BSA type}
and \eqref{eq: homogeneity for BPZ PDE system of BSA type}}\right\}\;\geq\;\;

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}\bigg(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{\infty}},\;\bigotimes_{j=0}^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}\bigg).

Differential equations at infinity

From the perspective of CFT,
the (PDE) part of Theorem
2.2

has the interpretation that
for
u\in\mathcal{H}_{\underline{{\lambda}}}
, the function
\mathcal{F}[u]

satisfies partial differential equations stemming
from degeneracies of primary fields at the
points
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
.
When we take
u
to moreover satisfy
K.u=q^{\lambda_{\infty}}\,u
,
it is natural to expect
the function
\mathcal{F}[u]
to satisfy a further
differential equation of order
\lambda_{\infty}+1
,
associated with the field at infinity.
It is possible to give a direct proof of this property from
the quantum group method, but such a proof is not entirely trivial.
By contrast, a very simple proof can be obtained as an application
of the results of this section.

For the statement, we introduce the following
differential operators at infinity

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{(\infty)}_{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{N}\left(x_{i}^{n+1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x_{i}}+(n+1)h(\lambda_{i})x_{i}^{n}\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{H}_{\underline{{\lambda}}}\cap\mathrm{Ker}(K-q^{\lambda_{\infty}})
,
\lambda_{\infty}\in\mathbb{N}
. The function
\mathcal{F}[u]
satisfies the differential equation

\mathscr{D}^{(\infty)}\,\mathcal{F}[u]=0
, where

\mathscr{D}^{(\infty)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda_{\infty}+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\geq 1\\
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{k}=\lambda_{\infty}+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-4/\kappa)^{\lambda_{\infty}+1-k}(\lambda_{\infty}!)^{2}}{\prod_{u=1}^{k-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{u}p_{i})(\sum_{i=u+1}^{k}p_{i})}\mathscr{L}^{(\infty)}_{p_{1}}\cdots\mathscr{L}^{(\infty)}_{p_{k}}.

Proof.

Note that
any
u\in\mathcal{H}_{\underline{{\lambda}}}\cap\mathrm{Ker}(K-q^{\lambda_{\infty}})

is a linear combination of
u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}
,
where
\underline{{\varsigma}}
ranges over the
\underline{{\lambda}}
-admissible sequences.
By linearity, it suffices to prove the statement for
u=u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}
.

To simplify, we make use of the fact that the representation
\mathsf{M}_{0}
is
the unit for tensor products of representations of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
,
as seen from (
2.1
).
Explicitly, we use the isomorphism

\displaystyle\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}\mathrm{id}_{\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}}\big)\otimes{\iota}_{\;{\lambda_{0}}}^{{\lambda_{0}},{0}}\;\colon\;\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}\big)\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{0}}\;\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\;\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}\big)\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{0}}\otimes\mathsf{M}_{0}.

We set

u^{\prime}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}=\big(\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}\mathrm{id}_{\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}}\big)\otimes{\iota}_{\;{\lambda_{0}}}^{{\lambda_{0}},{0}}\big)(u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}})
, and note that the function

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u^{\prime}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](z,x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})

satisfies the first order differential equation

0=\frac{\partial}{\partial{z}}\,\mathcal{F}[u^{\prime}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}]
by the (PDE) property
of Theorem
2.2
.
Therefore it is constant as a function of
z
.
When we specialize to
z=0
,
Theorem
5.9

states that this function is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u^{\prime}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](0,x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\;

\displaystyle\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},x_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,{\varsigma}_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{1}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{1}},x_{1})\,\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{0}\,0}^{\;{\varsigma}_{0}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}},x_{0})\,{\bar{w}}_{0}}.

On the other hand, by the (ASY) property
of Theorem
2.2

and the observation that
{B_{{\lambda_{0}},{0}}^{\;{\,\lambda_{0}}}=1}
,
we can determine the value of the constant, and we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[u^{\prime}_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](0,x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\;=\;\mathcal{F}[u_{\underline{{\varsigma}}}](x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}).

It now suffices to prove the differential equation for the above expression
involving a composition of intertwining operators.
Recall that
Q_{\lambda_{\infty}}^{\prime}\cong Q_{\lambda_{\infty}}
,
so
S_{\lambda_{\infty}}{\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{\infty}}^{\prime}=0
, and therefore

\displaystyle 0\;=\;\;

\displaystyle\Braket{S_{\lambda_{\infty}}{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{0}\,0}^{\;{\varsigma}_{0}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}},\bm{x}_{0})\,{\bar{w}}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\;\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\lambda_{\infty}+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\geq 1\\
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{k}=\lambda_{\infty}+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-4/\kappa)^{\lambda_{\infty}+1-k}(\lambda_{\infty}!)^{2}}{\prod_{u=1}^{k-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{u}p_{i})(\sum_{i=u+1}^{k}p_{i})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Braket{{\bar{w}}_{{\varsigma}_{N}}^{\prime},\;L_{p_{k}}\cdots L_{p_{1}}\,\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{N}\,{\varsigma}_{N-1}}^{\;{\varsigma}_{N}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{N}},\bm{x}_{N})\cdots\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{0}\,0}^{\;{\varsigma}_{0}}({\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}},\bm{x}_{0})\,{\bar{w}}_{0}}.

We can commute the Virasoro generators
L_{p_{m}}
to the right
by Corollary
4.7
in the
form (
5.6
) again.
For
p_{m}>0
we have
L_{p_{m}}{\bar{w}}_{\lambda_{0}}=0
, so after commutation we find
the desired differential equation.
∎

By contrast,
to prove Corollary
5.11
directly using the quantum
group method, one would have to introduce an auxiliary variable

x_{N+1}
and an auxiliary vector

\displaystyle u^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{\infty}}\otimes\big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{j}}\big)\otimes\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{0}}

as in
[
KP20
, Section 5.2]
,
conjugate by an appropriate exponentiatial of
\mathscr{L}^{(N+1)}_{1}
,
and take a complicated limit
x_{N+1}\to+\infty
.
The proof above is a significant simplification.

Full series expansions of the functions from the quantum group method

The property (ASY) in Theorem
2.2

gives the explicit leading coefficient of the Frobenius series expansion
of a function obtained by the quantum group method, in the
limit
x_{j}-x_{j-1}\to 0
.
The main result of this section
allows to express also all of the
higher order coefficients of this Frobenius series
as matrix elements of
compositions of intertwining operators
4
4

4

Specifically,
the method as presented in this section, applies to the
coefficients of the Frobenius series corresponding
to
x_{1}-x_{0}\to 0
.
The general case of expansions as

x_{j}-x_{j-1}\to 0
for
j>1
will be
obtained by associativity, which is addressed in the next section.
,
making their calculation tractable by
algebraic and combinatorial techniques.
This is perhaps the most important direct
application of the results of this section to the quantum group method.

6.
Associativity of intertwining operators

In this section we will prove one of the main results of the paper,
the associativity of the intertwining operators among the modules
of the first row subcategory.
This result is essentially the associativity in an appropriate
tensor category.

From the point of view of correlation functions of conformal field theories,
associativity amounts to the fact that the same correlation function
admits series expansions in different regimes, and the resulting
series represent the same function on the overlap of the domains where they
converge.
A geometric interpretation of this property is that different
pair-of-pants decompositions of the same Riemann surface can be used
interchangeably.

In VOA theory, associativity is a statement about the case
N=2
only,
but involving the full
intertwining operators, not merely a matrix element that gives
a particular correlation function.
Starting from the
N=2
case, it is then possible to inductively obtain
coincidence of various expansions of multipoint correlation functions
(or interchangeability of pair-of-pants decompositions of multiply
punctured spheres).

From the point of view of VOA intertwining operators,
we will be comparing a priori entirely different formal series,
and it is far from obvious that they should represent the same function.
Indeed, to obtain coincidence, we cannot separate a given
conformal block from the rest: the expansion of a single conformal
block in another regime will involve all possible conformal blocks,
so a carefully devised linear combination is needed.
The appropriate coefficients of the linear combination
will be the
6j
-symbols of the underlying quantum group.

A key subtlety is that the analytic functions represented by the
series are multivalued (when extended to their natural complex domains),
and the correct coincidence statements necessarily
involve branch choices. In our setup, the quantum group method and the
ordering of the variables on the real line leads to convenient
branch choices which facilitate the statement.
The multivaluedness, in turn, is closely related to braiding
properties, which feature crucially in the analysis of CFT correlation
functions, and form a key structure of the underlying tensor category.

In the previous sections, the quantum group has
been used mainly through the
fusion rules of the VOA modules, which matched the selection rules
for
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
representations. The selection rules themselves, however,
are not sensitive to the deformation parameter
q
.
By contrast, the results of this section will
involve the specific
6j
symbols, and they will lead to the specific
braiding properties. The associativity result therefore gives more profound
evidence for the equivalence of the first row subcategory of the
generic Virasoro VOA and the category of (type-one) finite-dimensional
representations of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
.

This section is organized as follows.
In Section
6.1
we introduce the setup
and give the precise formulation
of the associativity statement, Theorem
6.1
.
The proof of that statement is divided in two parts.
Section
6.2

contains the proof of a particular case involving a highest weight
matrix element.
In Section
6.3
, an inductive
construction starting from that particular case is used to
finish the proof of the general case.

6.1.
The setup and statement of associativity

Fix
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{\infty}\in\mathbb{N}
.
For arbitrary

w_{0}\in Q_{\lambda_{0}}
,

w_{1}\in Q_{\lambda_{1}}
,

w_{2}\in Q_{\lambda_{2}}
,

w_{\infty}^{\prime}\in Q_{\lambda_{\infty}}^{\prime}
,
and
{\varsigma}\in\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
,
we will consider the formal series

(6.1)

\displaystyle\Braket{w_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{2}\,{\varsigma}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}(w_{2},\bm{x}_{2})\,\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{1}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;{\varsigma}}(w_{1},\bm{x}_{1})\,w_{0}},

which is a special case
N=2
of the series in Section
5
,
and we will compare it with the formal series

(6.2)

\displaystyle\Braket{w_{\infty}^{\prime},\;\mathcal{Y}_{{\mu}\,\lambda_{0}}^{\;\lambda_{\infty}}\big(\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda_{2}\,\lambda_{1}}^{\;{\mu}}(w_{2},\bm{y})\,w_{1},\,\bm{x}\big)\,w_{0}}

for
{\mu}\in\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\cap\mathrm{Sel}(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{\infty})
.
The idea will be to substitute actual values
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
so that
